CAPTION: Stop trying to reinvent the wheel with your paid ads.

The fastest way to scale an app right now is to find what already prints and replicate the framework.

Here’s how to clone winning app ads in 5 minutes using AI UGC on Arcads:

1. Search TikTok for apps similar to yours
2. Find a viral video that’s already working
3. Upload it to Arcads and use the “Replace Actor” feature to match your exact demographic
4. Adapt the script and visual demo to your specific app

Done.

The best performing ads are always the ones that follow a format that’s already proven to convert.

Stop guessing. 
Replicate what works.

It has never been easier to distribute an app.

--- 2026-06-09 [TEXT] 9878 views
https://x.com/cesaralvarezll/status/2064393735887360057
CAPTION: Mascots are one of the best ways to make your app memorable and stand out from competitors.

I’ve been testing a workflow with Step 3.7 Flash to generate consistent mascots from a single sketch

The workflow is simple:
1. Sketch the mascot concept
2. Use a Python script to turn that sketch into detailed prompts
3. Run the workflow with Step 3.7 Flash to generate consistent character directions

From one sketch, I generated 4 mascot variations with different poses, expressions, and forms.

What I liked the most was the consistency, every expression felt like the same character, with almost no tweaking needed. And the prompts it generated were genuinely detailed, capturing tone and mood in a way that actually made the image generation work.

With this workflow, I can now create mascot versions for empty states, error screens and onboarding flows in minutes

- Makes you guess your screen time

Paywall
- Just one option: annual subscription
- 7 day free trial
- Shows what your progress could look like throughout the week

If your onboarding helps users understand the value they’ll get from your app, you’re already ahead of most products.

--- 2026-05-26 [IMAGE] 4855 views
https://x.com/cesaralvarezll/status/2059323931832504815
CAPTION: This Reddit user built 65 apps.

None were a huge success.

But by making a little money from each one ($65), he turned them into nearly $4.2k/month

Small apps compound.
SCREEN TEXT (image):
r/AppBusiness • hace 3 m
•••
• Less_Courage_3545
I built 65 "boring" apps. None of them went viral. (But together they
make ~$4,200/mo)
veryone on this sub is trying to build the next $1M ARR SaaS. I realized pretty early on that I didn't have the
marketing budget or the patience for that.
So, I took the exact opposite approach. I built a portfolio of tiny, boring, highly specific utility apps.
If you look at them individually, it's almost embarrassing. Most of them only make between $10 and $120 a
month. But here is the math: an average of $65/mo x apps = a a~$4,200/month side income.
When I tell people I have over 60 apps on the stores, they assume I work 100-hour weeks. The reality is that it's a
surprisingly low-effort model once you have the system down.
I don't run paid ads. I don't post on TikTok. I don't do content marketing. I rely completely on ASO (App Store
Optimization).
Here is my exact playbook:
1. The "Boring Keyword" Hunt I look for highly specific, long-tail keywords that have decent search volume but
terrible existing apps. Think "PDF compressor for X" or "Unit converter for Y".
2. The 48-Hour MVP I don't spend months on these. If an app takes me more than a few days to build, I drop
the idea. The app just has to do one thing slightly better than the outdated, ugly apps currently ranking for that
keyword.
3. Pure ASO I spend 80% of my time optimizing the title, subtitle, and screenshots. That's my only marketing. I
publish it, maybe tweak the keywords once a month, and otherwise forget about it.
My Stack (How I actually manage 65 apps) The obvious bottleneck here is speed and maintenance. You
cannot hand-code and manually deploy 65 apps from scratch and expect a decent ROl on your time, especially
when Apple and Google force SDK updates. To turn development and deployment into an assembly line, 1 use
Superapp Al + Revenue Cat + Claude sometimes. It basically removes all the friction of building, deploying, and
updating, so I can just focus on finding keywords and churning out the MVPs.
The Takeaway Stop looking for one unicorn. Sometimes it's easier to build a small herd of profitable donkeys.
If you are burnt out from trying to build a massive startup, try going micro. Happy to answer any questions in the
comments about my ASO process or how to find good keywords!
+ 1,1 mil B
D 218
& 7
~ Compartir

--- 2026-05-21 [TEXT] 2875 views
https://x.com/cesaralvarezll/status/2057542241212911768
CAPTION: Indie hacker tip if you're building apps and feel like you're constantly behind:

You're not behind. You're just reading the wrong stuff.

1/ Unfollow every AI news account on X.

They optimize for your anxiety, not your roadmap.

2/ Pick 2 topics. Not 10.

Mine: AI tools + mobile dev. If a link isn't one of those, I don't open it. Doesn't matter how viral it is. The dopamine of "keeping up" is the trap.

3/ Replace the algorithm.

X is optimized to make you anxious. 

I swapped my new tab page for a daily dev feed that lets me pick the tags. Two topics in, everything else filtered out. 30 seconds to set up, killed maybe 80% of the noise.

4/ Read 3 things. Close it.

The goal isn't to consume everything. It's to know what's real this week and get back to building.

Three solid articles > forty skimmed tweets. Every time.

5/ Save, don't read-now.

If it looks useful but it's not the 2 topics, bookmark it. 90% of the time you never go back and that's the point. It wasn't actually important.

6/ Build > learn.

The devs I see winning aren't the ones who read the most. They're the ones who read 20% as much and ship 5x more.

AI didn't make learning to code worthless. It made shipping the bottleneck. Optimize for that.

The whole point: you don't need to know everything. You need to know the 2 things that touch what you're building, and then close the tab.

CAPTION: Clandle has one of the best examples of good onboarding in an app.

Onboarding
- The first thing you see is a video demo → instant clarity on how the app works

CAPTION: The Symmetry app has implemented a range of onboarding strategies commonly used by top performing apps in the space.

Onboarding:
- Very long (+25 steps)
- Engaging animations and visuals
- Highly personalized experience
- Collects key user data early on
- Asks for a review during onboarding
- Builds relatability and clearly shows how the app can help the user

Soft Paywall:
- Monthly plan: €12.99
- Yearly plan with a 74% discount
- If you close the paywall, it re-offers the yearly plan
- If you close it again, it presents a “gift” with up to 85% off the yearly plan

Overall, this onboarding combines many of the most effective elements used by successful apps into a single flow

CAPTION: Duolingo is one of the best examples of good onboarding in an app.

Onboarding
- 7 questions to understand the user
- Creates a personalised learning plan
- Collects contextual data (like where the user hear about the app)
- Introduces key features like widgets

Paywall
- Smooth transition from onboarding to paywall
- The paywall feels like part of the experience, not a sales screen
- Personalised based on the language selected
- 7day free trial for the annual plan

One of the most important aspects of an app is onboarding, so make it as interactive as possible.

--- 2026-03-13 [IMAGE] 8519 views
https://x.com/cesaralvarezll/status/2032513612497969591
CAPTION: I checked the Opal app to see how they onboard users and here’s what I found:

Onboarding flow:
- Medium length onboarding (~12 steps)
- Asks about your mobile usage habits
- Shows relatable insights (ex: how many users share similar habits)
- Highlights the pain point (how many years you might be losing to screen time)
- Prompts for a review mid onboarding when excitement is high
- Demonstrates the value of the app (how many years you could save)

Soft paywall strategy:
- Expensive monthly plan (19,99€) with a 7 day free trial
- Yearly plan with a heavy discount (58%) and a 7 day free trial
- If you close the paywall, you’re offered a weekly plan for 4.99e€

Overall, this app is a great example of effective onboarding. 

It gathers key information about the user, highlights a relevant problem and then clearly shows how the app can help solve it.

--- 2026-03-11 [IMAGE] 29399 views
https://x.com/cesaralvarezll/status/2031830732759580702
CAPTION: I’m taking a look to the onboarding of Yaizo’s main competitor: Cal AI and I noticed a few clear patterns.

Onboarding flow:
- The first thing you see is a short demo video of the app
- Fewer steps than Yaizo but still a long onboarding (+15 steps)
- They ask where users discovered the app
- There is deep personalization during onboarding
- They ask for a review mid-onboarding
- A personalized plan is generated based on your answers

Soft paywall strategy:
- They encourage you to try it for free (technically an annual plan)
- The annual plan is heavily pushed with a 3 day free trial
- The monthly plan is available but not promoted

When multiple top apps share the same patterns, it’s usually a strong signal that those strategies work and that it’s worth considering implementing them in your own app

--- 2026-03-09 [TEXT] 7374 views
https://x.com/cesaralvarezll/status/2031072329892032812
CAPTION: Another consumer app that went viral through organic sharing is Wordle.

The concept was incredibly simple: every day, users try to guess a five letter word in five attempts.

What made it explode was how people shared their results.

The little grid of colored squares became instantly recognizable on social media.

Suddenly everyone was posting their score and comparing how many tries it took them.

It finally got sold to the New York Times for 7 figures.

Wordle didn’t need a marketing campaign. The users were the marketing.

--- 2026-03-09 [IMAGE] 15725 views
https://x.com/cesaralvarezll/status/2030976421884018977
CAPTION: Aside from changing the paywall and pricing, I made another change that significantly improved my conversion rate.

By watching user behavior through PostHog session replays, I noticed something interesting

Most users skipped the soft paywall and went straight to try the app’s main feature: the scanner.

But when they tried to save what they scanned, they hit a problem: they needed to create a collection first.

This created a lot of friction right at the moment when users were most excited about the product.

So I made a simple change: I moved collection creation into the onboarding and I also let users try the scanner.

This simple change a noticeable improvement in conversions.

If your app has an AHA moment, bring users to it during onboarding and remove as much friction as possible along the way.

CAPTION: Another great example of this is BeReal.

The idea of the app was simple: once a day, at a random time, you take a photo of what you're doing in that exact moment.

Because of its nature, the app created genuine and creative moments by default.

People started sharing their BeReals on Instagram and TikTok.

And without trying to force it, the product itself became the marketing.

The app went mega viral because the users were organically sharing the experience everywhere.

--- 2026-03-08 [IMAGE] 44090 views
https://x.com/cesaralvarezll/status/2030612023919210884
CAPTION: One of my apps was getting a good number of users starting the free monthly trial but they majority of them eventually canceled.

So I removed the free trial from the monthly subscription and replaced it with a discounted price for the first month. I kept the free trial only for the annual plan.

Since making this change, conversions have improved significantly.

Always remember to test different paywalls and pricing strategies

--- 2026-03-01 [IMAGE] 61302 views
https://x.com/cesaralvarezll/status/2028124145330823280
CAPTION: I downloaded the app to learn from their onboarding.

Here’s what I found 👇

Onboarding flow (60+ steps 🤯):
- Deep personalization: health data, eating habits, goals, lifestyle, etc
- They collect attribution data early (ex: “How did you hear about us?”)
- Mid-onboarding asks for a review (smart timing to boost ratings)
- At the end: a highly detailed, personalized plan built from your answers.

It’s long but every step increases commitment and perceived value.

Soft paywall strategy:
- Pushes 3-month and annual plan with heavy discount
- If you try to close the paywall → you unlock a “spin the roulette” discount
- First spin? Of course it lands on a weak discount
- They let you retry
- Second spin → highest discount.
- Up to 84% off the annual plan.

There’s a lot to learn here about driving higher conversions through onboarding: commitment loops, personalization, anchoring, and smart paywall psychology.

--- 2026-02-22 [IMAGE] 2059 views
https://x.com/cesaralvarezll/status/2025680625563537760
CAPTION: I shared a post on Reddit showcasing my new app.

Honestly, I was expecting some hate or random negative comments.

Instead, out of nowhere a QA engineer dropped a 700 word comment packed with feedback.

He tested EVERYTHING and gave me 12 incredibly valid, constructive improvement points.

Reddit users get a bad reputation sometimes (and sure sometimes it’s deserved) but there are genuinely helpful people on there like this one.
SCREEN TEXT (image):
PokeWeebBaka
•hace 58 min
Introduction screen... 5 clicks on the "Next" button, entering a name, 3 more clicks and boom a
monetization screen to "Purchase Pro access" before we have even had a chance to try out the app. Why
do people keep going down that route?
With that said, as someone who does QA as a career, there are some positive and negative things to think
about for the app to improve it. There is a feature request screen in the app, but I'm going to do that here.
1. Don't make monetization the first thing people see. If it was the end all be all of collection
tracking apps, maybe, but when it just gets released, it's not there yet. Is there an actual cost to the
number of scans someone does? I.E., does it cost the app creator when trying to recognize the card
images that makes it limited to 3 scans for free or unlimited when paid to justify the monetization?
2. Incorrectly labeled cards. Scanned a basic Miss. Valentine (Mikita) from OP14, and it labels it as the
dash pack version. They look similar, sure, one's full art and the other is not. However, scanned the
basic common leader Law OP14-001, and it picked the alt art version that's 100x the price. They look
nothing alike. I scanned other cards from the premium collection vol.5 set, and they drastically were
off. If it's not reliable, it's not useful.
3. Don card (Mihawk) from OP14 would not scan. Seems there are missing cards. It's understandable
for EB-03, as the app doesn't seem to have that set referenced, though the website advertises "All
Sets" and "All Decks". It also lists OP-14 as an "Other" set, rather than the booster packs. Lastly,
there are sets, decks, but no promotional cards?
4. If the camera stays open, I suppose you can bypass the 3 card per day limit. For what it's worth
though, the instant scanning of cards and response time is smooth.
5. When adding cards from the "Sets" menu, you have to add them one by one. You click on the card,
then you click on the "+" icon. Then it asks you how many and which collection to put the card in.
Then you have to go back, find the next one and repeat the process. There should be an option to
quick add cards. No asking for quantity, no extra taps. Swiping from one card to the next helps with
that. You have a similar action on the collection screen where you can edit cards and quickly
increase or decrease quantities. When adding a large collection initially, that is good because you
can't do that with 3 free scans per day.
6. Scrolling down on a card's details is also inefficient. The image is so large initially and you are unable
to scroll down if you start on the image itself. Since there's not "full size" image, making that a
reactive element is not helpful.
7. When clicking "Delete account" from the settings, you have to log in again. The Apple and Google
options either match the white background (impossible to read), or are very faint on the dark
background (hard to read)
8. On the home screen, the "latest Set" and "Most Expensive Cards" constantly bounce to the left after
a few seconds. That serves no purpose.
9. The cards show a "Market" and "Inventory" value. Not sure where those come from as they can vary
drastically depending on if you are using TCGPlayer, or Card Market. Neither of which matched the
card I was comparing against.
10. This is extremely minor, but the alignment of the navigation text is off. It's appearing as "--_-_--
11. The filter button doesn't work on the Collections screen. On the sets/decks, it does work, but could
use additional filter criteria instead of the owned/missing options that are also shown at the top of
the card lists. What if I want to search for all SPs, or all Alt Arts?
12. Overall the general Ul seems nice. There are just a few UX issues that add too much of a hinderance
to switch from other collection tracking apps.
3 & D Responder
So Premiar
~ Compartir
...

--- 2026-02-16 [IMAGE] 4621 views
https://x.com/cesaralvarezll/status/2023495691440714236
CAPTION: I downloaded the app.  

Here’s what I found:  

Bait on the onboarding: 
- Lets you select a photo of the mother and the father
- It "generates" the result
- Before showing anything, it asks for a 5-star review (asking for it on onboarding helps a lot to increase positive reviews)

“Soft” paywall:
- After the fake generation step, it redirects you to the paywall
- Weekly and Yearly plan
- You can close the paywall and browse the app but you can’t generate the photo unless you pay

it’s really a hard paywall disguised behind a baited onboarding flow. 

The weekly plan suggests they expect high churn.

This probably isn’t a long-term use app, so they optimize for short-term conversions over retention.

CAPTION: My last 7 days on X have been crazy.

I was getting barely any traction on my posts and then, out of nowhere, everything started picking up impressions.

Here’s what helped me:

- Share both achievements and failures
- Post genuinely useful content
- Publish >2x per day
- Communities are overrated
- Don’t be a reply bot, build real connections

At the end of the day, consistency pays off

CAPTION: Tools that I use to vibecode my iOS apps in less than a week:

- WrapFast: Swift boilerplate

- Firebase or Supabase: Auth, database and storage

- RevenueCat: Paywall, subscriptions and in app purchases

- Claude Code: AI coding

• Photopea + AppMakerKit: app icon and App Store Screenshots

--- 2026-01-10 [IMAGE] 8825 views
https://x.com/cesaralvarezll/status/2010027417029443997
CAPTION: I’m spending the weekend building an iOS app with zero mobile development experience.

Here’s exactly how I’m doing it👇

I’m using two tools: Antigravity and Claude Code.

The flow looks like this:

First, I use Gemini 3 Pro in Antigravity to plan the project.

I prompt explaining what I want:

“Create a detailed implementation roadmap for X app. Save it to an implementation_plan.md file.”

In this prompt I try to be as specific as possible: tech stack, share documentation links, design inspo, etc.

Once the plan exists, I switch to the terminal to use Claude Code.

It reads the plan Gemini created, understands it and executes it.

Then I just iterate from there.

My rule of thumb to save credits:

Questions & small, easy changes → Gemini 3 Pro

Executing the plan or building big features → Claude Opus 4.5

I will be sharing updates as the app evolves.

CAPTION: This hook is working insanely well for productivity & fitness apps:

“Day X of X doing X”

It’s an easy, organic way to promote a consumer app on social media without feeling like an ad.

Why it works:
• New Year’s resolutions
• Curiosity + accountability

Example:
“Day 1 of 30 getting fit” → seamlessly plug your fitness app

CAPTION: One simple trick to get AI to nail your layouts is to draw them yourself.

Instead of trying to explain a layout with words, just sketch it.

You can create proper wireframes in Figma, but that usually takes more time than necessary. 

A quick sketch is often more than enough.

In this case, I used Excalidraw.

Google's ownership and control of Chrome and Android-key methods for the
distribution of search engines to consumers-poses a significant challenge to effectuate a remedy
that aims to "unfetter [these] market[s] from anticompetitive conduct" and "ensure that there
remain no practices likely to result in monopolization in the future." Microsoft, 253 F.3d at 103.

dominance," Op. at 33, so that rivals may pursue distribution partnerships that this "realit[y] of
